6.
Implementing Realization Logic

We give an algorithm to compute a program from a proof outline in realizability logic.
It is a deductive verification that collects a set of verification conditions whose validity shows that the program is a solution to the synthesis task.
What is unconventional is that the validity has to be checked in the course of the verification condition computation.
The reason is that the validity checks steer the program construction, and also the verification conditions that will be collected in the future.
Despite these dynamics, we can show that a small number of verification condition checks will be sufficient to derive the program.
Moreover, the verification conditions compare ordinary predicates (rather than selections in the previous section), and therefore can be implemented as single SMT solver queries.
All this makes our algorithm efficient in practice.

Our algorithm implements the proof rules in realization logic, more precisely the non-backtracking strategy stated in
Theorem

4.4
.
Given a proof outline and a predicate in the postcondition, it traverses the proof backwards to determine suitable preconditions and programs that justify the postcondition.
Our algorithm is thus a function of type
\mathit{syn}:\mathit{prfOutls}\times\mathit{Predicates}\ \rightarrow\ \mathit{Predicates}\times\texttt{Programs}
.
It is defined in
Figure

10
and we discuss it in a moment.
An important feature of the function is to discard predicates from selections, in which case it may return
(\mathit{fail},-)
and an arbitrary program.

The function gives strong guarantees: when we start from a sketch that has been derived in realizability logic and a predicate in the postcondition, then the function is guaranteed not to fail but return a precondition and a program that, together with the given postcondition, will form a valid Hoare triple.
It is also very efficient: the number of verification conditions that have to be checked is linear, actually bounded by the size of the proof outline.

Theorem 6.1 (
\mathit{syn}
-Sound-And-Complete).

Consider
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
with
\mathit{s}\neq\mathit{fail}
.
Then
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})=(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog})

with
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
,
\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}
,
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}))
, and
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
The number of SMT solver calls is at most
|\texttt{po}|
.

Function
\mathit{syn}
is given in
Figure

10
.
If several cases apply, the topmost one will be taken.
This means the invocation will always return
(\mathit{fail},-)
on an empty precondition, and the
\mathit{fail}
predicate in a precondition will always be skipped.
When we have a command, we go through the predicates
\mathit{r}
in the precondition
until we find one that justifies the given postcondition
\mathit{s}
.
Since
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
and we started from a proof outline in realizability logic, the search for
\mathit{r}
is guaranteed to be successful.
Moreover, for the guarantees given by
Theorem

6.1
it does not matter which predicate
\mathit{r}
we take.
The notion of soundness in realizability logic gives the guarantee that the earlier (in the program text) assertions can handle every
\mathit{r}
.
But of course the choice has an influence on the shape of the program.

For a sequential composition
\texttt{po}_{1};\texttt{po}_{2}
, we take the given predicates
\mathit{s}
, propagate it through
\texttt{po}_{2}
to obtain
(\mathit{t},\texttt{prog}_{2})
, propagate
\mathit{t}
through
\texttt{po}_{1}
to get
(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1})
, and return
(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1};\texttt{prog}_{2})
.
Importantly, also here we have the guarantee that both calls will be successful.
When we have a non-terminal, we consult the proof outlines for the right-hand sides.
For an angelic choice among two right-hand sides, we first try to synthesize a program from the left proof outline, and if we fail we know that we will be successful on the right.
Whether the synthesis with the left proof outline will succeed can be foreseen by checking if the target post condition
\mathit{s}
is in the post condition of the left proof outline.

The involved cases are for demonic choices and loops.
These are the cases in which the program logics had to consider single predicates.
This is mimicked here, and we first discuss the demonic choice.
We go through all predicates
\mathit{r}
in the given precondition.
We try to construct proof outlines for both branches in which
\mathit{r}
is the precondition and
\mathit{s}
is the postcondition.
This is the task of the calls
\mathit{outl}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{1})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
and
\mathit{outl}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{2})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
.
We argue in a moment why these calls can actually be looked up in the given proof outline, and therefore mean no effort.
If one of these calls aborts, we continue with the next
\mathit{r}
.
Otherwise, we obtain the proof outlines
\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\texttt{po}_{2}^{\prime}
.
We invoke
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime},\mathit{s})
and
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{2}^{\prime},\mathit{s})
.
They will for sure return
\mathit{r}
as it is the only precondition, but we need the programs to be able to return
(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2})
.

For loops, we have to make an assumption on the form of the proof outline: the loop invariant should not be lost through weakening.
If assertions have to be weakened, we assume the proof outline takes the form
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Like in the previous case, we go through the predicates
i\in I
to find one that is stronger than the given postcondition
\mathit{s}
.
If it has been found, it remains to synthesize the program for the loop body.
We reconstruct the proof outline with
i
as the pre and postcondition, and rely on
\mathit{syn}
to determine the program.
If the proof outline takes the form

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
,
instead of returning an
i
of
I
, we return an
\mathit{r}
of
\mathit{R}
with
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}i
.

We elaborate on why the function calls
\mathit{outl}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
mean no overhead.
The point is that the Rules
(
DEM
)
and
(
LOOP
)
can only deal with single predicates in the precondition.
This means when we constructed the proof outline of interest,
we have constructed the proof outlines that we now need as a byproduct.
We just have to store them explicitly to be able to look them up now.
This explains the case of loops.

In the case of demonic choices, the proof outline construction may abort, and we explain how to handle this with hashing.
Since Rule
(
DEM
)
requires a singleton as the precondition, we must have done proofs
of the form

\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{i}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})+\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
From the requirements of Rule
(
DEM
)
, we also have the proof outlines

\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{i}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{j}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
for
j=1,2
.
The proofs might have been gathered to form the proof outline

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}+\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since the predicate
\mathit{s}
is in
\mathit{S}
, there must be a selection
\mathit{S}_{i}
with
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}_{i}
.
We can reuse the corresponding proof outlines

\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{i}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{j}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

by weakening the postcondition
\mathit{S}_{i}
to the singleton
\{\mathit{s}\}
.

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\emptyset\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle(\mathit{fail},-)

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{fail}\}\cup\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\cup\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}?}}}\;(\mathit{r},\texttt{com})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}:}}}\;\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{1};\texttt{po}_{2},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}let}}}\;\ (\mathit{t},\texttt{prog}_{2})=\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{2},\mathit{s})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}and}}}\;(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1})=\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{1},\mathit{t})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}in}}}\;(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1};\texttt{prog}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\texttt{N}(\texttt{po}),\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po},\mathit{s})

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{2},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}let}}}\;(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog})\in\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{1},\mathit{s})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}in}}}\;\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}?}}}\;(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}:}}}\;\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{2},\mathit{s})

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\cup\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\cup\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle\mathit{outl}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{i})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})=\mathsf{abort}\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}?}}}\;\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}:}}}\;

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}let}}}\;\ \texttt{po}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathit{outl}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{i})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}and}}}\;(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{i})=\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{i}^{\prime},\mathit{s})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}in}}}\;\ (\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\cup I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\cup I\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;:=\;\;

\displaystyle i\not\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}?}}}\;\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}:}}}\;

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}let}}}\;\ \texttt{po}^{\prime}=\mathit{outl}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}and}}}\;(i,\texttt{prog})=\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}^{\prime},i)\;\mathop{\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}in}}}\;(i,\texttt{prog}^{*})

Definition of the synthesis function.

6.1.
Example: Synthesizing Factorial Function

Recall the sketch
sketch
from last section’s example,
Figure

9
.
In the last section, we have proven
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
with
\mathit{R}=\{\texttt{x}=42\wedge\texttt{y}=1\}
and
\mathit{S}=\{\texttt{y}=42!\}
.
In this example, we will concretize the sketch to a program
prog
such that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
holds.
To do so, we use the function
\mathit{syn}
.
The whole proof outline is called
po
.
We use the same abbreviations as in the last example.
Additionally, with
T8
we abbreviate the full realizability triple of Line
8
.
That means
T8
stands for Lines
7
through
9
.
Other realizability triples are abbreviated similarly.
The realizability triple around the nonterminal is abbreviated by
TN
, i.e.
TN
stands for Lines
5
to
16
.
We abbreviate the selection of Lines
15
and
16
with
A_{15}
.
Other selections are abbreviated similarly.
Let
\{\mathit{r}\}=\mathit{R}
and
\{\mathit{s}\}=\mathit{S}
.
We start by calling

\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})
.
More specifically, the call is

\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;T22},\mathit{s})
.
The selection
I
is the singleton
\{i\}
.
The function invokes the call
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{T22},\mathit{s})
which returns
(i,\texttt{assume(x = 0)})
.
Next, it invokes
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)
which, in turn, invokes
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)
because
i\preceq_{d}i
holds trivially.
We can reuse the already done proof
\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
as
\texttt{po}^{\prime}
.
The call
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)

invokes
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}A_{15}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{x-{}-}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)
.
The call
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{A_{15}[0]\}\cup\{A_{15}[1]\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{x-{}-}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)
will invoke

\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{A_{15}[1]\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{x-{}-}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)

because the result of
\llbracket\texttt{x-{}-}\rrbracket(A_{15}[0])
is not more precise than
i
.
However, the call

\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{A_{15}[1]\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{x-{}-}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)

returns
(A_{15}[1],\texttt{x-{}-})
.
Here, we witness a foreshadowing on how the wrong angelic choice will be eliminated.
The eliminated predicate stems from the left production of the nonterminal which will not be able to produce the now selected predicate
A_{15}[1]
.
We continue the next recursive call of
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)
, i.e.

\mathit{syn}(\texttt{T4;TN},A_{15}[1])
.
It invokes
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{TN},A_{15}[1])

This call first checks whether the left branch can produce the target predicate.
This is not the case, so
eventually
(\mathit{fail},-)
is returned, eliminating the left branch.
The right branch is now considered, where
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{T12},A_{15}[1])
resolves to
(A_{5}[0],\texttt{y = y*x})
.
Therefore, the next call is

\mathit{syn}(\texttt{T4},A_{5}[0])
which resolves to the pair
(i,\texttt{assume (x>0)})
.
Getting back to the recursive call of the loop,
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{body}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle},i)

yields the pair
(\mathit{r},(\texttt{4;12;17})^{*})

because
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}i

with
\{\mathit{r}\}=\mathit{R}
.
In total, we get
(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog})=(\mathit{r},(4;12;17)^{*};\texttt{22})
as output.
We know that

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}

holds.
This means, the program indeed calculates
42
factorial.

7.
Application: Memory Management in Lock-Free Data Structures

We show how to employ realizability and realization logic to automatically generate code for the memory management in lock-free data structures.
This will also be the setting for our experiments.
There are several aspects that make this setting interesting for benchmarks: the states are type assignments and the semantics of commands is an abstract one.
So
SemGuS

(
Kim et al. 2021
)
is an ideal choice, while
SyGuS

(
Alur et al. 2013
)
and solver-aided languages
(
Solar-Lezama 2008
;
Torlak and Bodik 2013
)
need an encoding to capture the synthesis tasks.
The programs contain loops, which is difficult for
SyGuS
solvers.
Finally, there are many synthesis options, and the right ones may be far apart.
This creates a large space of potential solutions
(
Alur et al. 2018
)
that our compositional method manages to explore in a matter of seconds.

Safe Memory Reclamation

The asynchronous nature of lock-free data structures makes manual memory management difficult:
if the threads do not synchronize, how does a thread know that it is safe to free a memory cell, or safe to access it?
The solution is to add to the data structure a safe memory reclamation algorithm (SMR) which acts as a central instance that is informed about the intentions of all threads.
If a thread wants to access a memory cell, it asks the reclamation algorithm to protect the cell.
If it wants to free a memory cell, it asks the SMR to do so.
As the reclamation algorithm manages the protections, it can defer the free until it is safe.
Garbage collection is an SMR, but there are more efficient solution.
Epochs are a simple model
(
Fraser 2004
;
Harris 2001
)
, we consider here the hazard pointers that have been proposed for addition to the next C++ standard
(
Michael et al. 2023
)
.
To be precise, we support the elaborate version in which the first hazard pointer is stronger than the second.

Reclamation algorithms tend to implement lock-free data structures.
This means protection calls are non-atomic, and may be successful or fail due to interference.
The reclamation algorithm will not be able to determine success.
Instead, the thread that issued the protection call will have to find out by checking global invariants:
if, for example, a sentinal node has not changed when returning from the call, then the call was successful.
Unfortunately, finding the right combination of protection calls and checks has turned out error-prone.

Meyer and Wolff 2019
;
Meyer and Wolff 2020
gave a type system that checks memory safety.
It can be instantiated to different reclamation algorithms and applies to a variety of data structures.
We report on experiments with the queues MS and DGLM, and the sets ORVYY, Michael, VechevCAS, and VechevDCAS.
The type system is control-flow sensitive, which means the typing can be written as a proof outline in Hoare logic.
A type check on a program
prog
is successful, if the triple
\vdash_{t}\boldsymbol{\langle}\neg\mathit{fail}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{prog}\boldsymbol{\langle}\neg\mathit{fail}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
can be derived: starting from no assumptions about the protection of pointers, the program will not fail.
This means all pointers were protected before they were dereferenced.

Section

B.1
provides more background on
(
Meyer and Wolff 2019
;
Meyer and Wolff 2020
)
.
An important detail is that they use code annotations to inform the type system about protections that can otherwise otherwise only be inferred with a shape analysis.
These annotations have to be discharged separately.
We omit these checks in our experiments, and therefore our synthesis is only valid relative to the validity of the annotations.

Instantiation

We instantiate the parameters of our development.

Meyer and Wolff 2019
;
Meyer and Wolff 2020
track the protection of memory cells by assigning types from a set
\mathbb{T}
to the pointers in the program
Vars
.
Our states, predicates, and selections are thus

\mathit{States}=\textit{Vars}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
,
\mathit{Predicates}=\mathcal{P}(\textit{Vars}\rightarrow\mathbb{T})\cup\{\mathit{fail}\}
, and
\mathit{Selections}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\textit{Vars}\rightarrow\mathbb{T})\cup\{\mathit{fail}\})
.

Our implementation needs an assertion language to represent predicates and selections.
We use
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}=(\textit{Vars}\rightarrow\mathbb{T})\cup\{\mathit{fail}\}
and

\mathit{Selections}^{\#}=\mathcal{P}(\mathit{Predicates}^{\#})
.
The definition of the abstract predicates is due to
(
Meyer and Wolff 2019
;
Meyer and Wolff 2020
)
.
The first step is to apply a Cartesian abstraction on the predicates, which yields
\textit{Vars}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
.
A Cartesian predicate
\{\texttt{x}:\{\mathit{t}_{1},\mathit{t}_{2}\}\}\times\{\texttt{y}:\{\mathit{t}\}\}
represents the concrete predicate
\{\texttt{x}:\mathit{t}_{1}\wedge\texttt{y}:\mathit{t},\texttt{x}:\mathit{t}_{2}\wedge\texttt{y}:\mathit{t}\}
.
The second step is to exploit the fact that the types form a lattice, and represent

\texttt{x}:\{\mathit{t}_{1},\mathit{t}_{2}\}
by
\texttt{x}:\mathit{t}_{1}\sqcup\mathit{t}_{2}
.
The details are in
Section

B.5
.

The commands are the usual ones in
C
.
The semantics is from
(
Meyer and Wolff 2020
)
and defined in a way that works with the above abstraction.

Synthesis

Given a lock-free data structure and a reclamation algorithm, our goal is to synthesize in the data structure calls to the reclamation algorithm and code annotations that, together, guarantee memory safety, more precisely, make the above type check go through.

First, we enrich every line of code with a non-terminal from which calls to the SMR algorithm and invariant annotations may be generated. In the case of a single hazard pointer, for example, the

⬇

1

AC

::=

skip
;

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;|\;

2

atomic

{\
inv

active(v);}

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;|\;

3

(in:protect(v);re:protect(v);)

the non-terminal has the definition to the left.
For each variable
v
, we may issue a protection, add an annotation saying that the variable has not been freed, or simply skip the annotation.
For global invariants,

we use heuristics of where to put them, which improves the scalability.
The result of this phase is a sketch
sketch
.
Next, we try to prove
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\neg\mathit{fail}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\neg\mathit{fail}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
in realizability logic.
We use the algorithm from Section
5
and check the validity of the corresponding verification conditions.
If successful, we also get a proof outline that we feed to realization logic, implemented in the synthesis algorithm from Section
6
.
The algorithm has the guarantee to be successful, and returns a program that corresponds to the original code with suitable annotations added.
What is interesting is that we can control the synthesis function to avoid reclamation calls and code annotations whenever possible.
We give details on the implementation and its behavior on experiments.

8.
Evaluation

We implemented our synthesis algorithm in a
C++
program.
As we do not yet have an assertion language for selections,
our implementation targets the memory reclamation problem discussed above.
It can handle different reclamation algorithms and challenging data structures, though.
We give details on the implementation and elaborate on the performance, which was surprisingly good.

Implementation

The most important technique we implemented to improve the scalability is an optimistic variant of our approach.
The pessimistic variant is the one from
Section

7
that first validates the proof outline and then derives a program.
The optimistic variants derives the program without having validated the proof outline.
The point is that function
\mathit{syn}
checks the verification conditions it needs to construct the program anyhow, and therefore the output will be sound.
The verification conditions for proof outlines have to compare selections, and each such comparison may create quadratically many solver calls.

We moreover implemented the following application domain specific heuristics.
We assume local pointers do not need to be protected.
We only insert annotations
@inv active(v)
for sentinel nodes.
The point is that sentinel nodes will never be freed.
As discussed above, this would have to be confirmed by a check that we omit.
We automatically compute loop invariants from the predicates that are available when entering a loop.
There is no saturation nor widening.
Still, the approach worked for all data structures we examined.
For resolving non-terminals, we prefer
skip
over SMR calls and SMR calls over annotations.

Results

We experimented with the seven lock-free data structures and two hazard pointer variants discussed in
Section

7
.
The environment is an Apple M2 with 8GB RAM.
We do not have a solver backend but hand the bitvectors we need within our implementation.
The results are given in
Table

1
.
In some methods we used hints that can be deduced by a lightweight shape analysis.

The first column shows the maximum, average, and median size of a selection.
The next shows the number of invocations of the comparison function on predicates per comparison of selection.
For example,
\{a\}\preceq_{a}\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\}
may yield three comparisons
a\preceq_{d}b_{i}
.
This only applies to the pessimistic approach.
The next two columns display the time for the functions
\mathit{vc}
and
\mathit{syn}
in the pessimistic approach.
The last two columns are for the optimistic approach.
It clearly outperforms the pessimistic approach, but both are quite fast.

The key insight of our experiments is that there are two classes of synthesis options.
The options that are handled well by our approach lead to similar predicates and thus small selections, or they fail early.
The options that are difficult for our approach lead to new predicates that fail late.
This can be seen in the benchmarks with two hazard pointers.
In the set implementations, the order among the hazard pointers matters, and if we try to synthesize a protection with the wrong order we fail early.
For DGLM, the order does not matter and the synthesis time increases dramatically.

Taking a step back, the experiments indicate that our realizability and realization logics should be combined with an outer search
(
Alur et al. 2018
)
.
The search would concentrate on the synthesis options that are difficult for our approach and only fix them.
Our approach would handle the remaining, easier options.
We see this as a promising direction for future work.

9.
Related Work

We have already discussed the related work on
SemGuS
,
SyGuS
, and solver-aided programming in the introduction.
There is a body of work on programming models with angelic non-determinism, dating back to
Floyd 1967
.
The common case, however, is that the angelic choices are made at runtime, and therefore can react to the demonic choices.
These models are related to two-player zero-sum graph games
(
Grädel et al. 2002
)
typically used in reactive synthesis, as originally proposed by
Church 1957
and later developed by
Pnueli and Rosner 1989
.
In
SemGuS
, the angelic choices have to be made up-front, which adds a form of imperfect information
(
Reif 1984
)
that the common case does not have to deal with.
This imperfect information shines through in Rules
(
DEM
)
and
(
LOOP
)
, where the angelic player cannot react.
Two works, however, are closer to our development.

Mamouras 2016
presents a Hoare logic to reason about the correctness of programs with (runtime) angelic non-determinism.
His assertions are classical predicates, which means the proof construction is forced to decide on a synthesis option early on, may later fail due to incompatible decisions, and in this case has to backtrack.
To avoid precisely this backtracking, we proposed selections as assertions in realizability logic.
As a consequence, the resulting theories are largely different.

Celiku and von Wright 2003
consider the same class of programs as Mamouras, but try to refine angelic choices to conditionals and loops.
The refinement is guided by a proof outline, and may be compared to our realization logic.
An important difference is that they work with ordinary predicates, and try to synthesize appropriate ones.
We work with the new selections, instead, in which we have gathered the relevant predicates.

Also related is the recent
(
Miltner et al. 2022
)
that uses angelic non-determinism to synthesize recursive functions.
During recursive calls, they select an output value for the function and later check whether the choice can be justified.
The work
(
Srivastava et al. 2010
;
Jha et al. 2010
)
infers a program from verification conditions that contain unknown program parts which, in the case of
(
Jha et al. 2010
)
, should be filled by library components.
The crucial difference to realization logic is that we have eagerly analyzed the program space, and the task of
\mathit{syn}
amounts to a concretization rather than a search.
We have not seen a backtracking freedom guarantee in related work.

The deductive approach decomposes the task of synthesizing a program into synthesis tasks for subprograms
(
Manna and Waldinger 1980
)
.
A popular idea is to factorize the subprograms along input-output equivalence so that the computation can proceed with equivalence classes
(
Gulwani 2011
;
Albarghouthi et al. 2013
;
Udupa et al. 2013
;
Polozov and Gulwani 2015
;
Feser et al. 2015
;
Osera and Zdancewic 2015
)
.
What is different in our work is that we try to be eager in the bottom-up construction, and consider a set of synthesis options whose behavior does not have to match.
Related to our approach are
(
Wang et al. 2018
;
Drachsler-Cohen et al. 2017
)
that weaken the input-output equivalence by considering an abstract domain.
A solution for the abstract domain is then a restricted class of programs that can be searched more efficiently.
Realizability and realization logic may prove useful for this task.

We introduced our eager approach to reduce the number of costly verification queries, a
prominent endeavor in search-based synthesis
(
Alur et al. 2018
)
.
An important development is to inform the solver about semantic properties of the program sought
(
An et al. 2020
)
.
In the context of example-based specifications, a semantic property is the invariance to perturbations of the example set, which can be incorporated by enriching the example set.

Appendix A
Proofs

We present omitted proofs and details of this paper.

A.1.
Proofs for
Section

3

Lemma A.1.

The function
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket
is monotonic for all commands
com
, i.e. the following equation holds:

\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\ \implies\ \llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})\ .

Proof.

Let
\mathit{r}
and
\mathit{s}
be elements of
\mathit{Predicates}
with
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
.

Case 1:

\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})=\mathit{fail}
.
Then
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})
.

Case 2:

\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})\neq\mathit{fail}
.
Then,
\mathit{s}\neq\mathit{fail}
and there is no
p
in
\mathit{s}
with
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(p)=\mathit{fail}
.
Because
\mathit{s}\neq\mathit{fail}
, we have
\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}
and
\mathit{r}\subseteq s
.
Therefore, there also is no
p
in
\mathit{r}
with
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(p)=\mathit{fail}
.
Now, let
p^{\prime}
be an element of
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})
.
Thus, there exists a
p
in
\mathit{r}
with
p^{\prime}\in\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(p)
.
Since
\mathit{r}\subseteq s
, this
p
also is in
\mathit{s}
and therefore,
p^{\prime}
also is in
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})
, making
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})
a subset of
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})
.
In total,
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{s})
holds.
∎

Lemma A.2.

The relation
\preceq_{d}
is a partial order relation on
\mathit{Predicates}
.
This means, the order is reflexive, transitive and antisymmetric, i.e. the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathit{r}\in\mathit{Predicates}

\displaystyle\dv\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathit{r},\mathit{s},\mathit{t}\in\mathit{Predicates}

\displaystyle\dv\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\wedge\mathit{s}\preceq_{d}\mathit{t}\implies\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{t}

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathit{r},\mathit{s}\in\mathit{Predicates}

\displaystyle\dv\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\wedge\mathit{s}\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}\implies\mathit{r}=\mathit{s}

Proof.

Let
\mathit{r},s,t
be elements of
\mathit{Predicates}
.

Reflexivity (
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}
):

Case 1:
\mathit{r}=\mathit{fail}
. We get
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}
immediately.
Case 2:
\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}
. So
\mathit{r}
is a set of states.
Then,
\mathit{r}\subseteq r
holds.

Transitivity (
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\wedge\mathit{s}\preceq_{d}t\implies\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}t)
:

Case 1:
\mathit{t}=\mathit{fail}
. Then
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{t}
holds.
Case 2:
\mathit{t}\neq\mathit{fail}
. Then
\mathit{s}\neq\mathit{fail}
and
\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}
.
By transitivity of the relation
\subseteq
, we get
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{t}
.

Anti-symmetry (
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}\wedge\mathit{s}\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}\implies\mathit{r}=\mathit{s}
):

If either one of
\mathit{r}
and
\mathit{s}
is
\mathit{fail}
the other one also has to be fail for both inequalities to hold.
If neither one is
\mathit{fail}
, anti-symmetry follows from the anti-symmetry of
\subseteq
.
∎

Lemma A.3.

The partial order
(\mathit{Predicates},\preceq_{d})
is a complete lattice.
That means for any subset
\mathit{R}
of
\mathit{Predicates}
there exists a join and a meet.
In fact, the join and meet can be computed by the following equations:
Let
\mathit{R}
be a subset of
\mathit{Predicates}
.

\displaystyle\sqcup(\mathit{R})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathit{fail}\hphantom{\qquad\qquad}&,\mathit{fail}\in\mathit{R}\\
\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\mathit{r}&,\text{else}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\sqcap(\mathit{R})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathit{fail}&,\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{fail}\}\\
\bigcap_{\mathit{r}\in(\mathit{R}\setminus\{\mathit{fail}\})}r&,\text{else}\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\mathit{R}\subseteq\mathit{Predicates}

We show that
\sqcup(\mathit{R})
is an upper bound of
\mathit{R}
:
Let
\mathit{r}
be in
\mathit{R}
.
Case 1: Assume
\mathit{fail}\in\mathit{R}
.
Then
\sqcup(\mathit{R})=\mathit{fail}
.
Thus,
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\sqcup(\mathit{R})
.
Case 2: Assume
\mathit{fail}
is not in
\mathit{R}
.
Then
\sqcup(\mathit{R})=\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\mathit{r}
.
Thus,
\mathit{r}\subseteq\sqcup(\mathit{R})
and
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}\sqcup(\mathit{R})
.

We show that
\sqcup(\mathit{R})
is the least upper bound of
\mathit{R}
:
Let
u
be an upper bound of
\mathit{R}
.
Case 1: Assume
\mathit{fail}\in\mathit{R}
.
Then
\mathit{fail}\preceq_{d}u
.
Thus,
\sqcup(\mathit{R})\preceq_{d}u
.
Case 2: Assume
\mathit{fail}
is not in
\mathit{R}
.
Then
\sqcup(\mathit{R})=\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\mathit{r}
.
Since
u
is an upper bound,
\forall\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}:\mathit{r}\subseteq u
.
Thus,
(\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\mathit{r})\subseteq u
.
So,
\sqcup(\mathit{R})\preceq_{d}u
.

We show that
\sqcap(\mathit{R})
is a lower bound of
\mathit{R}
:
Let
\mathit{r}
be in
\mathit{R}
.
Case 1: Assume
\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{fail}\}
. Then
\mathit{r}=\mathit{fail}=\sqcap(\mathit{R})
.
Thus
\sqcap(\mathit{R})\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}
.
Case 2: Assume
\mathit{R}\neq\{\mathit{fail}\}
.
If
\mathit{r}=\mathit{fail}
, we get
\sqcap(\mathit{R})\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}
.
Otherwise,
\sqcap(\mathit{R})=\cap_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}\setminus\{\mathit{fail}\}}\mathit{r}
.
Therefore,
\sqcap(\mathit{R})\subseteq\mathit{r}
and thus
\sqcap(\mathit{R})\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}
.

We show that
\sqcap(\mathit{R})
is the greatest lower bound of
\mathit{R}
:
Let
l
be a lower bound of
\mathit{R}
.
Case 1: Assume
\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{fail}\}
. Then
\sqcap(\mathit{R})=\mathit{fail}
.
Thus
l\preceq_{d}\sqcap(\mathit{R})
.
Case 2: Assume
\mathit{R}\neq\{\mathit{fail}\}
. Let
\mathit{r}
be in
\mathit{R}\setminus\{\mathit{fail}\}
.
Then
l\subseteq\mathit{r}
.
This holds for every
\mathit{r}
.
Thus,
l\subseteq\sqcap(\mathit{R})
.
Therefore,
l\preceq_{d}\sqcap(\mathit{R})
.
∎

The Missing EMPTY Rule:

For completeness, we need to add the following rule to the calculus:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{}{\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\emptyset\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize({EMPTY})}}}

Proof of
Theorem

3.1

.

We prove soundness by structural induction over the proof tree.

Base Case:
We have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
COM
)
.
Thus, we have
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
.
Therefore, the realizability triple
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.

Induction Step:

In the first case, we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
SEQ
)
.
Thus, we have the realizability triples
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
By applying the induction hypothesis, we get
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Let
\mathit{t}
be an element of
\mathit{T}
.
Then, there exists a predicate
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
and a program
\texttt{prog}_{2}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch}_{2})
with
\models_{d}\{\mathit{s}\}\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{t}\}
.
And, there exists
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
and
\texttt{prog}_{1}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch}_{1})
with
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
By completeness of Hoare logic, we get

\vdash_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}\{\mathit{s}\}
and

\vdash_{d}\{\mathit{s}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}\{\mathit{t}\}
.
Using rule
(
SEQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1};\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{t}\}
.

In the next case we have the triple
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
CSQ
)
.
Thus we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
with
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{S}^{\prime}\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
Through the induction hypothesis, we get
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Now, let
\mathit{s}
be an element of
\mathit{S}
.
Since
\mathit{S}^{\prime}\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
, there is an
\mathit{s}^{\prime}
in
\mathit{S}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{s}^{\prime}\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
.
From
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
we know, that there is an
\mathit{r}^{\prime}
in
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
and a program
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch})
such that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}^{\prime}\}
holds.
Through transitivity of the relation
\preceq_{d}
, we also get that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
holds.
Due to monotonicity of executions, and since
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
holds, there is an
\mathit{r}
in
\mathit{R}
for which
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
is true.
That means,
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.

In the next case, we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
DEM
)
.
Therefore, we know
\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{r}\}
and the realizability triples
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
From the induction hypothesis we know that
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Now, let
\mathit{s}
be an element of
\mathit{S}
.
Then, there exists
\texttt{prog}_{1}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch}_{1})
for which
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}\{\mathit{s}\}
is true.
Analogously, there exists
\texttt{prog}_{2}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch}_{2})
for which
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
is true.
We now show that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
is true.
This directly implies that

\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

holds.
Let
ex
be an execution of
\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}
.
Thus, it is either an execution of
\texttt{prog}_{1}
or
\texttt{prog}_{2}
.
W.l.o.g. let
ex
be in
\mathit{exec}(\texttt{prog}_{1})
.
Since
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}\{\mathit{s}\}
is true, we also get that
\llbracket\texttt{ex}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})
is more precise than
\mathit{s}
.
Therefore, the Hoare triple
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
is true and thus

\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.

In the next case, we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
LOOP
)
.
Thus we know the realizability triple
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds and
\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{r}\}
.
The induction hypothesis yields
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
From this we know that there is a
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch})
with
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{r}\}
.
We now show that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{*}\{\mathit{r}\}
is true.
For this, we inductively show
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{i}\{\mathit{r}\}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\texttt{prog}^{0}
is
skip
,
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{0}\{\mathit{r}\}
immediately follows.
In the induction step, we show
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{i}\texttt{;prog}\{\mathit{r}\}
.
From the hypothesis, we know that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{i}\{\mathit{r}\}
and
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{r}\}
hold.
Referring to the case where we have proven sequences in this proof, this implies that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{i+1}\{\mathit{r}\}
is true.
Thus, we know that
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{*}\{\mathit{r}\}
holds.
Therefore,
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
is true.

In the next case, we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
ANG
)
.
From this, we know
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{rhs}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds with
\texttt{N}\texttt{::= rhs}\mid\dots
.
From the induction hypothesis we get
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{rhs}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since
\mathit{drv}(\texttt{rhs})
is a subset of
\mathit{drv}(\texttt{N})
, we get
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
immediately.

In the last case, we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through the rule
(
GATHER
)
.
From this, we know
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Let
\mathit{s}
be an element of
\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}
.
W.l.o.g. let
\mathit{s}
be an element of
\mathit{S}_{1}
.
From the induction hypothesis we know that there is an
\mathit{r}
in
\mathit{R}_{1}\subseteq\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}
and a program
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch})
with
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
Thus, we get
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
This concludes the soundness proof.

We proceed with the proof for completeness.
In the first case, the postcondition is empty.
We have
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\emptyset\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using the rule
(
EMPTY
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\emptyset\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

In the second case, the postcondition is not empty.
We have
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Thus, for every
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
there is a program
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{sketch})
and a predicate
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
for which

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
Since Hoare logic is complete, we have

\vdash_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
We can mimic this proof in realizability logic for
sketch
by using rule
(
ANG
)
mimicking the derivation of
prog
from
sketch
.
Thus, we have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{r}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{s}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since
\mathit{S}
is finite, we only need to do the proof for finitely many programs.
These proofs are then combined using the rule
(
GATHER
)
multiple times to derive

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
for the subset
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
of
\mathit{R}
containing the used predicates
\mathit{r}
of
\mathit{R}
.
Then, using rule
(
CSQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
∎

A.2.
Proofs for
Section

4

To properly define the notation of a valid proof outline, we state a
a proof system for that ranges over proof outlines in
Figure

11
.
When we write
\vdash_{p}\texttt{N}(\texttt{po})
we mean

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\texttt{po})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
where
\mathit{R}
resp.
\mathit{S}
are the union of all pre- resp. postconditions of the subproofs in
po
.
The function
\mathit{sketch}
extracts the program sketch out of the proof outline:

Figure 11.
Definition of the Program Logic for Synthesis over proof outlines. Extensions of traditional Hoare logic are
blue
.

\footnotesize\frac{\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(r)\preceq_{d}s}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{r\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{s\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PCOM})}}\frac{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PSEQ})}}\frac{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{r}\}}}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PLOOP})}}\frac{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathit{R}=\{r\}}}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PDEM})}}\frac{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}R^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}S^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathit{S}^{\prime}\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PCSQ})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{rhs}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\texttt{N}::=\texttt{rhs}\;|\;\dots}{\vdash_{p}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{rhs}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PANG})}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\emptyset\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PEMPTY})}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{1})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{2})}{\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}_{2})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{{\scriptsize({PGATHER})}}}

Definition 0.

The function
\mathit{gather}
combines two proofs over the same sketch.

\mathit{gather}:(\mathit{prfOutls}\times\mathit{prfOutls})\rightharpoonup\mathit{prfOutls}

\displaystyle\mathit{gather}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle})=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

\displaystyle\mathit{gather}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{p1}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{p2}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle})=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{p1},\texttt{p2})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

\displaystyle\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})=

\displaystyle\,\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})\texttt{;}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1}+\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}+\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})=

\displaystyle\,\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})+\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})=

\displaystyle\,\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})\;|\;\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathit{gather}(\texttt{N}(\texttt{po}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{2}),\texttt{N}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}))=

\displaystyle\,\texttt{N}(\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})\;|\;\texttt{po}_{2}\;|\;\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})

In the last case,
\texttt{po}_{1}
and
\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}
are the productions where the right hand side in both proof outlines are the same.
The proof outlines
\texttt{po}_{2}
and
\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}
reason over different sketches and their proof outlines therefore cannot be combined.

In order for the function
\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}_{2})
to be defined, it is required that
\texttt{po}_{1}
and
\texttt{po}_{2}
are proofs over the same sketch, i.e.
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{1})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{2})
.

We need the following theorem:

Theorem A.5 (Equivalence).

The following implication holds for any proof
po
of
\mathit{prfOutls}
and any angels
\mathit{R}
and
\mathit{S}
:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

The following implication holds for any sketch
sketch
of
Sketches
and any angels
\mathit{R}
and
\mathit{S}
:

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\exists\,\texttt{po}\in\mathit{prfOutls}:\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})=\texttt{sketch}\,\wedge\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Proof of
Theorem

A.5

.

We start with the first implication.
We show the implication by structural induction over the proof tree.

Base Case:
:
We have
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through
(
PCOM
)
.
Thus, we know
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
is true.
With rule
(
COM
)
we directly get
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Induction Step:

Since all induction steps are only applying the induction hypothesis and rebuilding the proof in the other proof system, we demonstrate it on one case only.
We have the proof outline
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through
(
PSEQ
)
.
With the preconditions and the induction hypothesis we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}R\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}S\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}S\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}T\boldsymbol{\rangle}
where the sketches are of the corresponding proof.
Using the rule
(
SEQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}R\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}T\boldsymbol{\rangle}

We continue with the second implication.
Again, we show the implication by structural induction over the proof tree.

Base Case:
:
We have
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through
(
COM
)
.
Thus, we know
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
is true.
With rule
(
PCOM
)
we directly get
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Induction Step:

Since all induction steps are only applying the induction hypothesis and rebuilding the proof in the other proof system, we demonstrate it on one case only.
We have the realizability triple
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
through
(
SEQ
)
.
With the preconditions and the induction hypothesis we get two proofs
\texttt{po}_{1}
and
\texttt{po}_{2}
for which the following holds:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Furthermore,
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{i})=\texttt{sketch}_{i}
.
With rule
(
PSEQ
)
we get
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}R\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}T\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
∎

We show several properties of a valid proof outline:
When we write
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}
with
\texttt{po}=\texttt{po}_{1}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\texttt{po}_{n}
we mean
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{n}
.

Lemma A.6.

In a sketch proof outline, all realizability triples hold, i.e. the following implications are true:

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}_{2}\implies\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\mathit{S}=\mathit{S}^{\prime}

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{1}+\texttt{po}_{2}\implies\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\mathit{R}=\mathit{R}^{\prime}\wedge\mathit{S}=\mathit{S}^{\prime}

\vdash_{p}\texttt{N}(\texttt{po})\implies\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge I=I^{\prime}\,\wedge\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}I\wedge I\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}\ .

Proof.

We use structural induction to prove the implications.
We proof one implication at a time.

For the first one, the only rules that need to be considered are
(
PSEQ
)
,
(
PCSQ
)
and
(
PGATHER
)
.
The base case holds trivially.
Rule
(
PSEQ
)
immediately yields the sought after result.
Through transitivity of
\preceq_{a}
,
(
PCSQ
)
also yields the implication after applying the induction hypothesis.
When rule
(
PGATHER
)
is applied, the proof has the following shape:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\cup\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\cup\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\cup\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{2},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\cup\mathit{T}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

We can use the gather rule to show
\vdash_{p}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}_{i},\texttt{po}_{i}^{\prime})
because the individual proofs hold according to the induction hypothesis.
That fact that the intermediary assertions match is a result of the induction hypothesis.

The other cases all follow the same pattern:
The proofs for the rule corresponding to the program (here
(
PSEQ
)
for a program sequence) and rule
(
PCSQ
)
hold trivially.
For rule
(
PGATHER
)
, the rule itself has to be applied to the result of the induction hypothesis, as seen here.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.1

.

With the newly introduced notation, the theorem is the following:

Theorem A.7 (Soundness).

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}
and
\texttt{po}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\texttt{po}^{\prime}
together imply

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}
.

We conduct the proof using structural induction over the proof tree in realization logic.

Base Case:

We know that
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}R\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}S\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\boldsymbol{\langle}R\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}S\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{r\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{s\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

hold.
From the rule
(
RCOM
)
of the realization logic we know that
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(r)\preceq_{d}s
is true.
This directly implies that
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{r\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{s\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.

Induction Step:

Consider the rule
(
RSELECT
)
.
The implication immediately holds because the nested proofs are sound per
Lemma

A.6
.

Consider the rule
(
RAC
)
.
Again, since the nested proofs are sound, we get

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{1}
and
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}
and
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{2}
.
Applying the induction hypothesis on the precondition of the rule yields the triple

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}

Applying rule
(
PANG
)
on each subproof, yields

\vdash_{p}N(\texttt{po}_{1})
and
\vdash_{p}N(\texttt{po}^{\prime})
and
\vdash_{p}N(\texttt{po}_{2})
.
Applying rule
(
PGATHER
)
multiple times yields the sought after result:

\vdash_{p}N(\texttt{po}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}^{\prime}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{2})
.

Consider rule
(
RSEQL
)
.
Since the nested proofs are sound, we get
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and also
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{2}
.
The induction hypothesis yields the realizability triple

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using rule
(
PSEQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}_{2}
.

Consider rule
(
RSEQR
)
.
Again, by soundness of the subproofs we get
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and also

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying the induction hypothesis we get
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since
\mathit{S}\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}
, the infer rule can be applied to the first realizability triple resulting in

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

Then applying rule
(
PSEQ
)
yields:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Consider rule
(
RDEM
)
.
Again, through the soundness of the sub proofs (
Lemma

A.6
), we get
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{p}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
The induction hypothesis yields
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
with
\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}=\{\mathit{r}\}
.
Applying rule
(
PDEM
)
results in

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}+\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Consider rule
(
RLOOP
)
.
Through the soundness of the sub proofs (
Lemma

A.6
), we get the realizability triple
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying the induction hypothesis, we get the realizability triple
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}I^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}I^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
with
I^{\mathsf{w}}=\{i\}
.
Applying rule
(
PLOOP
)
yields
the realizability triple

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}I^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime*}\boldsymbol{\langle}I^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Consider rule
(
RTRANS
)
.
The induction hypothesis yields soundness of
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{2}
.
Applying the induction hypothesis again yields the sought after result:
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}_{3}
.

Consider rule
(
RCSQ
)
.
The induction hypothesis yields

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using rule
(
PCSQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Lastly, consider rule
(
RGATHER
)
.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
with
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})
.
Applying the rule
(
PGATHER
)
, we get

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.3

Again, we state theorem with the new notation:

Theorem A.8.

\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}
and
\vdash_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}
and
\texttt{po}\preceq_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}
together imply
\texttt{po}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\texttt{po}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For this proof, we conduct a structural induction over the syntactic structure of the proof
po
.
Then, some cases require their own additional structural induction over the proof tree of
\texttt{po}^{\prime}
in the proof outline calculus.
In the following, we also have
X\preceq_{a}X^{\mathsf{w}}
for any selections
X
and
X^{\mathsf{w}}
.

Base Case:

\texttt{po}=\texttt{com}
.
Then,
\texttt{po}^{\prime}=\texttt{com}
.
We show
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
by induction over the proof tree of
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
In the base case, we have the realizability triple
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{r}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{s}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Thus, we know that
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}

holds.
Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.
In the induction step, there are two cases. Either the rule
(
PGATHER
)
was used, or the rule
(
PCSQ
)
was used.
If
(
PGATHER
)
was used, we have
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
From this, we know that
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using the rule
(
RGA
)
, we get
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
If
(
PCSQ
)
was used, we have
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
From the precondition, we know
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.
Using the induction hypothesis, we get
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying rule
(
RCSQ
)
yields
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Induction Step:

If the proof is a sequence, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ ,

then the other proof is

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Thus we know that, the realizability triples

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis, we get
\texttt{po}_{2}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}
.
Applying the rule
(
RSEQR
)
yields

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Because
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds, the realizability triple
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
also holds due to the consequence rule.
We also have that
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
is true.
Applying the induction hypothesis, we get
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using rule
(
RSEQL
)
yields

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Combined with the rewrite from before and rule
(
RTRANS
)
, we have

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

In the next case, the proof is a demonic choice, i.e.

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Then, the other proof is

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

We proceed by induction over the proof tree of

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

If the proof was done with the rule
(
PDEM
)
, it has the following shape:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

with
\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}=\{\mathit{r}\}
.
With the overall induction hypothesis and because the subproofs are sound, we know that
