\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

hold.
Using the rule
(
RDEM
)
, we get

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Next, we, again, have two possibilities.
The proof was conducted using
(
PGATHER
)
or
(
PCSQ
)
.
Starting with rule
(
PGATHER
)
, the proof has the following shape:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup\mathit{R}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup\mathit{S}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

From preconditions of the gather rule we get

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{2}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and
Applying the induction hypothesis then yields

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Similarly for
\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\texttt{po}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.
Using the rule
(
RGATHER
)
we get

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1},\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{gather}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1},\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Closing with the rule
(
PCSQ
)
, the proof has the following shape:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

From the precondition of the infer rule, we have

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Using the induction hypothesis, we get the following rewrite:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Now, the rule
(
RCSQ
)
can be applied to get the sought after result:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

This concludes the proof for demonic choices.

In the next case, the proof is a loop.
This case is analogous to demonic choices.

Finally, consider the proof is over a nonterminal.
So, the first proof is

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{r})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Then, the other proof can either also be a weaker proof over a nonterminal or it is only one production of the nonterminal.
We start with the first case.
The second proof is then

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

We know that all the subproofs hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis then yields the following rewrites for all
i
:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

We apply the rule
(
RANG
)
on the rewritten part several times.
Together with rule
(
TRANS
)
, we combine the rewrites and get:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{r})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Moving on to the case where one production is chosen.
Without loss of generality, let this be the first production.
The proof therefore is

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since we know that

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds, we can apply the induction hypothesis and get the rewrite

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Then, we apply rule
(
RAC
)
and get the following:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup(\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup(\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Furthermore, the rule
(
RSELECT
)
can be applied to get

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup(\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\cup(\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Lastly, applying the rule
(
RTRANS
)
yields the sought after result:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

This concludes the proof.
∎

We proof the following lemma:

Lemma A.9.

The following implication holds:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\mathit{S}\preceq_{a}\{\mathit{s}\}\implies\exists\,\mathit{r},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1},\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\dv\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}\wedge\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\\
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\texttt{po}_{1}\preceq_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\texttt{po}_{2}\preceq_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\,\wedge\\
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{1})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1})\wedge\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{2})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2})\ .

Proof.

We conduct the proof by induction over the proof tree of

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

The base case holds trivially.

In the first case of the induction step,
the proof was done using the rule
(
PDEM
)
.
Then, we have

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and also

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
with
\mathit{R}=\{\mathit{r}\}
.
Thus, we know
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using the rule
(
PCSQ
)
on both proofs, we get the realizability triples

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

We have two remaining cases, either the proof was finished using the rule
(
PGATHER
)
or rule
(
PCSQ
)
.
In the first case, we have

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

Since
\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}\preceq_{a}\{s\}
, one assertion
\mathit{S}_{i}
is stronger than
\{\mathit{s}\}
.
Without loss of generality, let this be
\mathit{S}_{1}
.
From the above realizability triple, we know that the realizability triple

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

holds. Applying the induction hypothesis, we get an
\mathit{r}
and the proofs
\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\texttt{po}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
for which the realizability triples

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

hold.

In the second case, we have

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

The precondition of the rule
(
PCSQ
)
requires

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

Applying the induction hypothesis yields an
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}
and

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\mathsf{w}}
there is an
\mathit{r}^{\prime}
of
\mathit{R}
with
\mathit{r}^{\prime}\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}
.
Thus we can strengthen the precondition of the realizability triples to get

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
∎

We proof the following lemma:

Lemma A.10.

The following implication holds:

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge i\in I\ \implies\ \exists\,\texttt{po}^{\prime}\dv\,\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\\
\texttt{po}\preceq_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\wedge\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime})\ .

Proof.

The proof is analogous to one of the previous lemma.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.4

The proof is done by an induction over the structure of the
prf
.
Again, we first state the theorem using the new notation:

Theorem A.11 (Backtracking Freedom).

Let
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
.
Then there are
\mathit{r}
and
\texttt{po}^{\prime}
so that

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
,

\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
,
and
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime})\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}))
.

Proof.

Base Case:

The proof is
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying soundness yields a predicate
\mathit{r}
with
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
and
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
.
Therefore, we can apply rule
(
RCOM
)
and get
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Induction Step:

If the proof is a sequence, we have

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Then, we know that the subproofs are true so
\texttt{po}_{1}
and
\texttt{po}_{2}
hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis on the second realizability triple from above, we get an
\mathit{s}
with
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
and a proof
\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}
for which the following holds:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{t}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Next, we apply the induction hypothesis on the other sequence and get a predicate
\mathit{r}
with
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
and a proof
\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}
for which the following holds:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Applying rule
(
RSEQR
)
, we get the following rewrite:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{t}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Then, applying rule
(
RSEQL
)
, we get the following rewrite:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{t}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{t}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Finally, with rule
(
RTRANS
)
, we get the sought after result:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{T}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{;}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{t}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

If the proof is a demonic choice, so we have

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ ,

and a predicate
\mathit{s}
with
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
.
Then we can apply
Lemma

A.9
to get
a predicate
\mathit{r}
and the proofs

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

with
\texttt{po}_{i}\preceq_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}
.
.
Using
Theorem

4.3
, the following two rewrites are sound:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

An application of the rule
(
RDEM
)
then yields the sought after result:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Moving on with loops.
We have the proof

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and a predicate
\mathit{s}
with
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
.
Since
I\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
, there also is an
i
of
I
with
i\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
.
Applying
Lemma

A.10
, we get a proof
\texttt{po}^{\prime}
for which

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

holds and
\texttt{po}\preceq_{p}\texttt{po}^{\prime}
.

Theorem

4.3
then yields

\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Applying rule
(
RLOOP
)
yields

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Since
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}I
, we know there is an
\mathit{r}
in
\mathit{R}
for which

\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}^{\#}i
holds.
Applying rule
(
RCSQ
)
, we get the sought after result:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{r\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle})^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{s\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

In the last case, we consider proofs over nonterminals.
We have a predicate
\mathit{s}
and the proof

\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

with
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
.
Since
(\mathit{S}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\mathit{S}_{n})=\mathit{S}
,
there is a selection
\mathit{S}_{i}
with
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}_{i}
.
Without loss of generality, let this
i
be
1
.
We know the realizability triple
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.
Using the induction hypothesis, we get the following for an
\mathit{r}_{1}
of
\mathit{R}_{1}
:

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{1}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Using the rule
(
RANG
)
, we get the following rewrite:

\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\\
\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{1}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\ .

Then, with rule
(
RSELECT
)
, we get

\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{1}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{1}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Finally, applying rule
(
RTRANS
)
twice, we get

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{n}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\sim$}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}_{1}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Because
\mathit{R}_{1}\subseteq\mathit{R}
, we have
\mathit{r}_{1}\in\mathit{R}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

A.3.
Proofs for
Section

5

We show the following lemma:

Lemma A.12.

The function
\mathit{sp}
is monotonic (if the specifications of the nonterminals are monotonic):

\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\ \implies\ \mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch})\ .

Proof.

We proof this lemma by induction over the structure of sketch.

Base Case:

We show
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
implies
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{com})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{com})
.
Let the predicate
s
be of
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{com})
.
Then,
s=\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r}^{\prime})
for some predicate
\mathit{r}^{\prime}
of
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
.
Since we know
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
, there is a predicate
\mathit{r}
in
R
with
r\preceq_{d}\mathit{r}^{\prime}
.
Due to the interpretation of commands being monotonic, we get that
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(r)\preceq_{d}\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r}^{\prime})=s
.
Because
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(r)
is in
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{com})
,

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{com})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{com})
follows.

Induction Step:

First, we consider the case where the sketch is a sequence.
We show
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
implies
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})
.
From the induction hypothesis, we know that
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1})
holds.
Applying the induction hypothesis again yields

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}),\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1}),\texttt{sketch}_{2})\ .

Inserting the definition of the strongest post function, we get

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})\ .

Next, we consider the case where the sketch is a choice.
We show

\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\implies\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\ .

Let the predicate
s_{j}
be of
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})
.
Thus, there is an
\mathit{r}^{\prime}
of
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
with
s_{j}^{\prime}=s_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup s_{2}^{\prime}
and

s_{1}^{\prime}
is of
\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})
and

s_{2}^{\prime}
is of
\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})
.
Since
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
, there is an
\mathit{r}
of
R
with
\{\mathit{r}\}\preceq_{a}\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\}
.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields

\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\text{ and }\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}^{\prime}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})

Therefore, there is an
s_{1}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})
and an
s_{2}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})
with
\{s_{1}\}\preceq_{a}\{s_{1}^{\prime}\}
and
\{s_{2}\}\preceq_{a}\{s_{2}^{\prime}\}
.
Therefore,
s_{j}=s_{1}\sqcup s_{2}\preceq_{d}s_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup s_{2}^{\prime}=s_{j}^{\prime}
.
Since
s_{j}
is of
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})
, we have

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\ .

Next, consider the sketch is a loop.
We show

\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\implies\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I])\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I])\ .

Let
i
be of
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I])
.
Thus,
\mathit{R}^{\prime}\preceq_{a}\{i\}
.
By transitivity we have
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\{i\}
and thus
i
is also in
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I])
.
The inequality
i\preceq_{d}i
holds trivially.

Lastly, consider the sketch is a nonterminal.
Monotonicity follows directly from the required monotonicity of the specification.

This concludes the proof.
∎

We show the following lemma:

Lemma A.13 (Soundness).

For any
\mathit{R}
of
\mathit{Selections}
and any sketch
sketch
of
Sketches
, the following realizability triple holds (if the specifications of the nonterminals and loops are sound):

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Proof.

We proof this lemma by induction over the structure of the sketch.

Base Case:

We show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{com})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
For every
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
we can can show
the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using the rule
(
GATHER
)
we can show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\{\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
By definition, this is the same as

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{com})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Induction Step:

First, consider a sequence of sketch.
We show the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Applying the induction hypothesis twice yields the realizability triples:

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\text{ and }\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}),\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Using rule
(
SEQ
)
, we get the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}),\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

This is the same as the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}\texttt{;}\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}

.

Next, consider the sketch is a choice.
We show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

From the definition of the strongest post, we know that it is the following:

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})=\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\{\mathit{s}_{1}\sqcup\mathit{s}_{2}\mid\mathit{s}_{1}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\wedge\mathit{s}_{2}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\}\ .

For every
\mathit{r}
of
\mathit{R}
, we know from the induction hypothesis that

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Using the rule
(
CSQ
)
, we can show
the realizability triples

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}_{k1}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

for any
\mathit{s}_{k1}
of
\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})
and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}_{k2}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

for any
\mathit{s}_{k2}
of
\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})
.
Let
\mathit{s}_{j}
be the join of any two
\mathit{s}_{k1}
and
\mathit{s}_{k2}
.
Using the rule
(
CSQ
)
, we can show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}_{j}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
With the rule
(
DEM
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}_{j}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying rule
(
GATHER
)
yields

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}_{1}\sqcup\mathit{s}_{2}\mid\mathit{s}_{i}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{i})\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Applying rule
(
GATHER
)
once more yields

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\bigcup_{\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}}\{\mathit{s}_{1}\sqcup\mathit{s}_{2}\mid\mathit{s}_{i}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{i})\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

This is the same as

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Next, consider the sketch is a loop.
Let
i
be of
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}^{*})
.
From soundness of the invariant annotation, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
With rule loop, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
With gather, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}^{*})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}^{*})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

With
(
CSQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}^{*})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Finally, consider the sketch is a nonterminal.
Soundness follows directly from the required soundness of the specification of the nonterminal.

This concludes the proof.
∎

We show the following corollary:

Corollary 0.

For any
\mathit{R},\mathit{S}
of
\mathit{Selections}
and any sketch
sketch
of
Sketches
with sound annotations, the following implication holds:

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}\ \implies\ \vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Proof.

Lemma

A.13
yields
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying rule
(
CSQ
)
gives us the sought after result

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2

.

We have already shown soundness above.
We prove completeness by an induction over the proof tree of
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Base Case:

We show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{com})\preceq_{a}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
From the precondition of rule
(
COM
)
, we know
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
.
Therefore
\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{com})=\{\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\}\preceq_{a}\{\mathit{s}\}
.

Induction Step:

Consider the rule
(
SEQ
)
.
We show
that the validity of

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}T\boldsymbol{\rangle}
implies

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}T
.
From the preconditions we know that both

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}T\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
and

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{S},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}T
.
Because the strongest post function is monotonic we get the sought after inequality

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}T
.

Consider the rule
(
DEM
)
.
We show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}\ .

From the preconditions we know
that

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}

hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields the inequalities

\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}

and

\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
Let the predicate
s
be an element of
S
.
Because the strongest posts are more versatile than
S
, there is an
s_{i}
of each strongest post with
s_{i}\preceq_{d}s
.
Therefore,
s
is an upper bound of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
and thus

s_{1}\sqcup s_{2}\preceq_{d}s
holds.
To conclude this case, see that
s_{1}\sqcup s_{2}\in\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})
,
and therefore

\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}

holds.

Consider the rule
(
ANG
)
.
Completeness follows directly from the requirement that the specification of the nonterminals is complete.

Consider the rule
(
CSQ
)
.
We show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}^{\prime}\ .

From the preconditions, we know that the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds with

\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{R}^{\prime}
and

\mathit{S}\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}^{\prime}
.
From the induction hypothesis we know that

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}^{\prime},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
The sought after result immediately follows from monotonicity and because the relation
\preceq_{a}
is transitive:

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}^{\prime}
.

Consider the rule
(
LOOP
)
.
We show
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I]\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
implies

\mathit{sp}(\{i\},\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I])\preceq_{a}\{i\}
.
Due to the precondition of rule
(
LOOP
)
and completeness of the annotation, we have
i\in I
.
Thus,
i\in\mathit{sp}(\{i\},\texttt{sketch}^{*}[I])
and therefore the inequality trivially holds.

Lastly, consider the rule
(
GATHER
)
.
We show

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\implies\mathit{sp}(\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}(\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i})\ .

From the preconditions we know that

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R}_{i},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}_{i}
.
Using monotonicity of the strongest post function, we get

\mathit{sp}(\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}_{i}
for every
i
.
Together, this means

\mathit{sp}(\cup_{i}\mathit{R}_{i},\texttt{sketch})\preceq_{a}(\cup_{i}\mathit{S}_{i})

This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.1

.

We prove soundness by induction over the structure of the sketch.

Base Case:

We show
\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle})\implies\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Because the verification conditions hold, we know that the inequality
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{com})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
is true.
The realizability triple
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
immediately follows from
Corollary

A.14
.

Induction Step:

In the first case, the sketch is a sequence.
We know the following verification conditions hold:
\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
.
Thus, we know that the verification conditions

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle})

and

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle})

also hold.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\text{ and }\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\ .

Using the rule
(
SEQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

If the sketch is a choice, we have

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}

and for every
r\in\mathit{R}
we have

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle})

and

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle})
.
Applying the induction hypothesis yields
the two realizability triples

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}

for every
r\in\mathit{R}
.
Using rule
(
CSQ
)
, we can show the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}

and

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using the rule
(
DEM
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}

for every
r\in\mathit{R}
.
Using the gather rule, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Applying the rule
(
CSQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}+\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

If the sketch is a loop, we have the verification conditions

\models(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{s}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}))
from the check annotation function
and the inequality

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{s}^{*}[I])\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
The induction hypothesis yields the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{s}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
for every
i\in I
.
Applying the rule
(
LOOP
)
yields

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
for every
i\in I
.
Since
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{s}^{*}[I])
is a subset of
I
we can use multiple applications of the rule
(
GATHER
)
to get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{s}^{*}[I])\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{s}^{*}[I])\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Per definition, we have
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{s}^{*}[I])
.
Using the rule
(
CSQ
)
, we get that
\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.

If the sketch is a nonterminal,
we know that
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{{N}}[\Gamma])\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
holds and from the check annotations function we know there is a
j
with
\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{prog}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog})\boldsymbol{\rangle})
for every
prog
of the oracle function
\mathbb{O}(\texttt{N},j)
.
Also, we have
(\bigcup_{\texttt{prog}\in\mathbb{O}(\texttt{N},j)}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog}))\preceq_{a}\Gamma(\mathit{R})
.
Using the induction hypothesis and rule
(
ANG
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
for every
prog
of
\mathbb{O}(\texttt{N},j)
.
Using the gather rule, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}\boldsymbol{\langle}\bigcup_{\texttt{prog}\in\mathbb{O}(\texttt{N},j)}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Using the rule
(
CSQ
)
, we get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}\boldsymbol{\langle}\Gamma(\mathit{R})\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Since
\Gamma(\mathit{R})
is equal to
\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{N}[\Gamma])
, we can apply
(
CSQ
)
one more time to get

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Next, we show completeness.
First, see that if
\mathit{S}\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}
and
\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
then we also have

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}^{\mathsf{w}}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
.
We proceed by induction over the structure of sketches.

Base Case:

We have
\texttt{sketch}=\texttt{com}
.
Completeness results from completeness of
\mathit{sp}
.

Induction Step:

We have
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
From soundness of the strongest post and the induction hypothesis, we get
\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle})
and we also get the verification conditions of

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1};\texttt{sketch}_{2})\boldsymbol{\rangle})
.
Because of completeness of the strongest post and our remark that the post condition in verification conditions may be weakened, we get

\models\mathit{vc}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{sketch}_{1})\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{sketch}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle})
.

The proof for choices directly follows from soundness and completeness of the strongest post function.
Then, only an application of the induction hypothesis is left.

In the case of loops we can discharge the first inequality by soundness and completeness from strongest post.
The inequalities from the check annotation function are true because the annotations are sound and thus the induction hypothesis can be applied.

The case for non-terminals is more cumbersome.
The first inequality follows from completeness of the strongest post function.
We now discuss why the inequalities in the check annotations function hold:
From
\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}[\Gamma]\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
, we get for every
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
a program
prog
and an
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{S}
for which

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
holds.
This implies that

\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{r}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{prog}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{\mathit{s}\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.
From completeness of the strongest post, we get

\mathit{sp}(\{\mathit{r}\},\texttt{prog})\preceq_{a}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
By monotonicity of the strongest post function, we get

\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog})\preceq_{a}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
We collect the programs returned from completeness in the set
\mathit{P}
.
We get that
(\cup_{\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{P}}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog}))\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
Since
S
is finite,
\mathit{P}
is finite.
Thus, there is a
j
for which
\mathit{P}\subseteq\mathbb{O}(\texttt{N},j)
holds.
With this, we also have

(\cup_{\texttt{prog}\in\mathbb{O}(\texttt{N},j)}\mathit{sp}(\mathit{R},\texttt{prog}))\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
The verification conditions on the individual programs hold because of the soundness of the strongest post function and the induction hypothesis.
∎

A.4.
Proofs for
Section

6

Proof of
Theorem

6.1

First, we state the theorem using the new notation:

Theorem A.15 (
\mathit{syn}
-Sound-And-Complete).

Consider
\vdash_{p}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
and
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
with
\mathit{s}\neq\mathit{fail}
.
Then
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})=(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog})

with
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
,
\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}
,
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}))
, and
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
The number of SMT solver calls is at most
|\texttt{po}|
.

Proof.

We proof this theorem by induction over the shape of
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.

Base Case:

We have the valid realizability triple
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Thus, we know for every
\mathit{s}^{\prime}\in\mathit{S}
there is an
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
with
\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathit{s}
is of
\mathit{S}
, the call
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{com}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})
eventually terminates returning
(\mathit{r},\texttt{com})
.
The predicate
\mathit{r}
cannot be
\mathit{fail}
because
\mathit{s}
is not
\mathit{fail}
and
com
preserves
\mathit{fail}
.
Also
\texttt{com}\in\mathit{drv}(\texttt{com})
holds trivially.
Moreover, since

\llbracket\texttt{com}\rrbracket(\mathit{r})\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
, the Hoare triple
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{com}\{\mathit{s}\}
holds.
Since we only have to go through
\mathit{R}
once to find a suitable
\mathit{r}
, the number of SMT solver calls is at most
|\mathit{R}|\leq|\texttt{po}|
.

Induction Step:

In the first case,
we have
\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1};\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Thus, the call is
\mathit{syn}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1};\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle},\mathit{s})
.
Let
\mathit{T}
be the intermediary selection.
Applying the induction hypothesis, the call
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{2},\mathit{s})
returns
(\mathit{t},\texttt{prog}_{2})
with
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{T}
,
\mathit{t}\neq\mathit{fail}
,
\texttt{prog}_{2}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{2}))
, and
\models_{d}\{\mathit{t}\}\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
Also the number of SMT calls is at most
|\texttt{po}_{2}|
.
Then we call
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}_{1},\mathit{t})
and by the induction hypothesis we get

(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1})
with
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
,
\mathit{r}\neq\mathit{fail}
,
\texttt{prog}_{1}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{1}))
, and
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}\{\mathit{t}\}
.
Also the number of SMT calls is at most
|\texttt{po}_{1}|
.
Put together, the function returns
(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{1};\texttt{prog}_{2})
.
Since both Hoare triples hold, we get

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1};\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
Also, we have
\texttt{prog}_{1};\texttt{prog}_{2}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}))
.
And the number of SMT calls is at most
|\texttt{po}_{1}|+|\texttt{po}_{2}|\leq|\texttt{po}|
.

In the next case, we have

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{N}(\texttt{po})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
We have two sub-cases:
First,
po
is only one proof outline as opposed to many separated by the
\;|\;
symbol.
Then the pre and post condition of
po
and
\texttt{N}(\texttt{po})
match.
We directly get the sought after result by applying the induction hypothesis and seeing that
\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}))\subseteq\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{N}(\texttt{po})))
.
In the second sub-case, we know that
\texttt{po}=\texttt{po}_{1}\;|\;\texttt{po}_{2}
.
Since
\mathit{S}
is the union of all posts of
po
, we know that eventually we try out a subproof with
\mathit{s}
in its post.
Then, the induction hypothesis is applied again directly yielding the required results.

In the next case, we have

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{1}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}+\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}_{2}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
Because the proof outline is valid, we know that for every
\mathit{s}\in\mathit{S}
there is an
\mathit{r}
of
\mathit{R}
for which there is a program
\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}
for which

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
holds.
This implies that

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{i}\{\mathit{s}\}
must hold.
This also means,
the realizability triple

\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{r}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{prog}_{i}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{s}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds which in turn implies that

\models_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{r}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{drv}(\texttt{po}_{i})\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{s}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
holds.
By completeness, the outlines
\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}
can be built when supplied with the correct
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
.
Then, we use the induction hypothesis
to get
(\mathit{r},\texttt{prog}_{i})
from the recursive calls with
\mathit{r}\in\{\mathit{r}\}\subseteq\mathit{R}
, and the Hoare triple

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{i}\{\mathit{s}\}
, and
\texttt{prog}_{i}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}))
,
and the number of SMT solver calls for each recursion is at most
|\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}|
.
Since
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{i})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i})
we also have that

\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}
is of
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}_{1}+\texttt{po}_{2})
.
Because
\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{i}\{\mathit{s}\}
, we also have

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}_{1}+\texttt{prog}_{2}\{\mathit{s}\}
.
When the proof outlines
\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{i}
are properly looked up, there is no need for additional SMT calls.
Thus, the SMT calls are at most
|\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{1}+\texttt{po}^{\prime}_{2}|\leq|\texttt{po}_{1}+\texttt{po}_{2}|
.

In the last case consider a loop

\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{R}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}\texttt{po}\boldsymbol{\langle}I\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{*}\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathit{S}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
.
In the case the realizability triple of the loop was weakened, we keep the original invariant selections.
Thus
\mathit{R}
resp.
\mathit{S}
are stronger resp. weaker than
I
.
We have
I\preceq_{a}\mathit{S}
.
Therefore, there is an
i
of
I
with
i\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
.
This
i
will eventually be found by the
\mathit{syn}
function.
Since the proof is sound,
I
is a sound invariant for
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})
.
Thus, for every
i
of
I
, the realizability triple

\vdash_{a}\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})\boldsymbol{\langle}\{i\}\boldsymbol{\rangle}
is valid.
Therefore, the proof outline
\texttt{po}^{\prime}
can be constructed.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathit{syn}(\texttt{po}^{\prime},i)
returns
(i,\texttt{prog})
with

\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}))
, and

\models_{d}\{i\}\texttt{prog}\{i\}
, and the number of SMT solver calls is at most
|\texttt{po}^{\prime}|
.
Because

\models_{d}\{i\}\texttt{prog}\{i\}

holds, we also have

\models_{d}\{i\}\texttt{prog}^{*}\{i\}
.
Since
\mathit{R}\preceq_{a}I
there is an
\mathit{r}
of
\mathit{R}
with
\mathit{r}\preceq_{d}i
.
Since
i\preceq_{d}\mathit{s}
, we can weaken to

\models_{d}\{\mathit{r}\}\texttt{prog}^{*}\{\mathit{s}\}
and return
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
.
Because
\texttt{prog}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime}))
, we have

\texttt{prog}^{*}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime})^{*})
.
Because
\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po}^{\prime})=\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})
, we have

\texttt{prog}^{*}\in\mathit{drv}(\mathit{sketch}(\texttt{po})^{*})
.
Because the proof outline
\texttt{po}^{\prime}
can be looked up and does not need recomputing,
we need at most
|I|
additional SMT calls.
All in all, we have at most
|\texttt{po}|
SMT calls.
∎

A.5.
Proofs for
Section

7

Definition 0.

An abstract predicate is fail or maps variables to sets of states of the SMR automaton.

\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}=(\textit{Vars}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{O}))\cup\{\mathit{fail}\}

To substitute the original more precise relation
\preceq_{d}
defined on
\mathit{Predicates}
, we introduce a new relation on the abstracted domain.

Definition 0.

We define a stronger relation
\preceq_{d}^{\#}
on
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.

a\preceq_{d}^{\#}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ b=\mathit{fail}\vee\forall\,v\in\textit{Vars}\dv a(v)\subseteq b(v)

Ignoring the predicate
\mathit{fail}
, this relation matches the order in Meyer and Wolff’s paper.

Definition 0.

We define a predicate abstraction function to abstract the original predicate and a predicate concretisation function to concretize abstract predicates.
The predicate abstraction function is a cartesian abstraction:

\alpha_{d}:\mathit{Predicates}\rightarrow\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}

Let
v
be a variable in
Vars
. Then, the function is defined as follows:

\begin{cases}\alpha_{d}(r)=\mathit{fail}&,r=\mathit{fail}\\
\alpha_{d}(r)(v)=\bigcup_{q\in r}q(v)&,\text{else}\end{cases}

We continue with the demon concretisation function:

\gamma_{d}:\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}\rightarrow\mathit{Predicates}

\gamma_{d}(a)=\begin{cases}\mathit{fail}&,a=\mathit{fail}\\
\{q\in\mathit{States}\mid\forall v\in\textit{Vars}\dv q(v)\subseteq a(v)\}&,\text{else}\end{cases}

Lemma A.19.

r\preceq_{d}s\implies\alpha_{d}(r)\preceq_{d}^{\#}\alpha_{d}(s)\ .

Proof.

If the predicate
s
is
\mathit{fail}
, then
\alpha_{d}(s)
is also
\mathit{fail}
so the inequality holds.
Otherwise, we have

\displaystyle r\subseteq s\implies

\displaystyle\,(\bigcup_{q\in r}q(v))\subseteq(\bigcup_{q\in s}q(v))

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\,\alpha_{d}(r)\preceq_{d}^{\#}\alpha_{d}(s)\ .

Here,
v
is any variable of
Vars
.
∎

Lemma A.20.

a\preceq_{d}^{\#}b\implies\gamma_{d}(a)\preceq_{d}\gamma_{d}(b)\ .

Proof.

If the abstract predicate
b
is
\mathit{fail}
, then
\gamma_{d}(b)
is also
\mathit{fail}
so the inequality holds.
Otherwise, we have

\displaystyle a(v)\subseteq b(v)\implies

\displaystyle\,\{p\in\mathit{States}\mid\forall v^{\prime}\in\textit{Vars}\dv p(v)\subseteq a(v)\}\subseteq

\displaystyle\qquad\{p\in\mathit{States}\mid\forall v^{\prime}\in\textit{Vars}\dv p(v)\subseteq b(v)\}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\,\gamma_{d}(a)\preceq_{d}\gamma_{d}(b)\ .

Here,
v
is any variable of
Vars
.
∎

Lemma A.21.

The pair
(\alpha_{d},\gamma_{d})
is a Galois connection between
\mathit{Predicates}
and
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
:

(\mathit{Predicates},\preceq_{d})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.57222pt}{\hbox to29.61946pt{$\smash{-}\mkern-7.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\smash{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-7.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.01563pt}{\hbox to29.61946pt{$\mathord{\leftarrow}\mkern-7.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\smash{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-7.0mu\smash{-}$}}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\enskip\raisebox{-4.73608pt}{\ $\scriptstyle\alpha_{d}$\ }}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\hskip 5.49913pt\raisebox{7.50104pt}{\ $\scriptstyle\gamma_{d}$\ }}\hss}\rule[-5.79718pt]{0.0pt}{15.88153pt}\hskip 29.61946pt}(\mathit{Predicates}^{\#},\preceq_{d}^{\#})

This means, the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\forall r\in\mathit{Predicates}

\displaystyle\dv r\preceq_{d}\ \gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}

\displaystyle\dv\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))\preceq_{d}^{\#}\ a

Proof.

We start the proof with the first equation.
Let
r
be of
\mathit{Predicates}
.
If
r=\mathit{fail}
then
\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))=\mathit{fail}
.
Thus, the inequality
r\preceq_{d}\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))=\mathit{fail}
holds.
If
r
is not
\mathit{fail}
, then

\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))=\{p\in\mathit{States}\mid\forall v\in\textit{Vars}\dv p(v)\subseteq\bigcup_{q\in r}q(v)\}\ .

Let the state
q
be of
r
.
Then
q
is also in

\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))
.
Therefore
r\subseteq\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))
.
And that means
r\preceq_{d}\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))
.

We continue with the second equation.
Let
a
be of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.
If
a
is
\mathit{fail}
, then
\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))
is trivially stronger.
If
a
is not
\mathit{fail}
, then we show that for any
v
of
Vars
the inclusion

\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))(v)\subseteq a(v)
holds.
Let
v
be of
Vars
.
Then

\displaystyle\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))(v)=

\displaystyle\,\cup_{q\in\gamma_{d}(a)}q(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\cup_{q\in\{p\in\mathit{States}\mid\forall v^{\prime}\in\textit{Vars}\dv p(v^{\prime})\subseteq a(v^{\prime})\}}q(v)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\,a(v)\ .

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.22.

The relation
\preceq_{d}^{\#}
is a partial order relation on
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.

Proof.

We first show reflexivity.
Let
a
be of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.
If
a=\mathit{fail}
, then the inequality
a\preceq_{d}^{\#}a
trivially holds.
Otherwise, let
v
be a variable of
Vars
.
Then the inclusion
a(v)\subseteq a(v)
also holds.

We continue with transitivity.
Let
a
,
b
and
c
be of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.
We assume that the inequalities
a\preceq_{d}^{\#}b
and

b\preceq_{d}^{\#}c
are true.
If
c=\mathit{fail}
, then the inequality
a\preceq_{d}^{\#}c
trivially holds.
Otherwise, we get that
b\neq\mathit{fail}
and also
a\neq\mathit{fail}
.
Let
v
be of
Vars
.
We have
a(v)\subseteq b(v)
and
b(v)\subseteq c(v)
.
Through transitivity of the relation
\subseteq
we also get the inclusion
a(v)\subseteq c(v)
.

Lastly, we show antisymmetry.
Let
a
and
b
be of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.
We have
a\preceq_{d}^{\#}b
and
b\preceq_{d}^{\#}a
.
If
a
is
\mathit{fail}
then
b
must also be
\mathit{fail}
and vice versa.
If neither one is fail, let
v
be of
Vars
.
We know that the inequalities
a(v)\subseteq b(v)
and
b(v)\subseteq a(v)
are true and thus we have
a(v)=b(v)
for all
v
of
Vars
.
That means,
a
and
b
are equal.
∎

Lemma A.23.

The partial order
(\mathit{Predicates}^{\#},\preceq_{d}^{\#})
is a complete lattice.
In fact, the join and meet can be computed by the following equations:
Let
A
be a subset of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sqcup(A)=\mathit{fail}\hphantom{\ \,\quad\qquad\qquad}&,\mathit{fail}\in A\\
\sqcup(A)(v)=\bigcup_{a\in A}a(v)&,\text{else}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sqcap(R)=\mathit{fail}&,R=\{\mathit{fail}\}\\
\sqcap(R)=\bigcap_{a\in(A\setminus\{\mathit{fail}\})}a(v)&,\text{else}\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
A
be a subset of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.
Let
a
be an element of
A
.
We first show that the join is an upper bound
a\preceq_{d}^{\#}\sqcup(A)
.
If
\mathit{fail}\in A
, then
\sqcup(A)=\mathit{fail}
so
the inequality

a\preceq_{d}^{\#}\sqcup(A)

trivially holds.
Otherwise, let
v
be of
Vars
.
The inclusion
a(v)\subseteq\bigcup_{a^{\prime}\in A}a^{\prime}(v)
holds trivially, since
a\in A
.
Now, to show that the join is the least upper bound, let
u
be an upper bound of
A
.
If
u
is
\mathit{fail}

the inequality
\sqcup(A)\preceq_{d}^{\#}u
holds trivially.
Otherwise, if
u
is not
\mathit{fail}
, we know
\mathit{fail}\not\in A
.
Let
v
be of
Vars
.
For any abstract demon
a
of
A
,
a(v)\subseteq u(v)
must hold.
Therefore the inclusion
\sqcup(A)(v)=\bigcup_{a\in A}a(v)\subseteq u(v)
holds.
Thus,
the inequality

\sqcup(A)\preceq_{d}^{\#}u
is true.

Moving on with the meet.
Let
a
be an element of
A
.
We first show that the meet is a lower bound:

\sqcap(A)\preceq_{d}^{\#}a
.
If
A=\{\mathit{fail}\}
, then
a=\mathit{fail}
so the inequality

\sqcap(A)\preceq_{d}^{\#}a
trivially holds.
Otherwise, let
v
be of
Vars
.
Then, by definition we know that
\sqcap(A)(v)=\bigcap_{a^{\prime}\in A\setminus\{\mathit{fail}\}}a^{\prime}(v)
.
If
a
is
\mathit{fail}
, the inequality trivially holds.
Otherwise,

\sqcap(A)(v)\subseteq a(v)
is also true.
Now, to show that the meet is the greatest lower bound, let
l
be a lower bound of
A
.
If
l
is
\mathit{fail}
,
A
must be
\{\mathit{fail}\}
and thus
\sqcap(A)=\mathit{fail}
, so
l\preceq_{d}^{\#}\sqcap(A)
trivially holds.
Otherwise, let
v
be of
Vars
.
Then for any
a
of
A
that is not
\mathit{fail}
,
l(v)\subseteq a(v)
must hold.
Thus,
l(v)\subseteq\bigcap_{a^{\prime}\in A\setminus\{\mathit{fail}\}}a^{\prime}(v)=\sqcap(A)(v)
holds.
And therefore the inequality
l\preceq_{d}^{\#}\sqcap(A)
is true.
∎

Next, we lift the abstract predicates to abstract selections.
We remind the reader, that selections are sets of predicates, i.e.
\mathit{Selections}=\mathcal{P}(\mathit{Predicates})
.

Definition 0.

An
Abstract Selection
is a set of abstract predicates.

\mathit{Selections}^{\#}=\mathcal{P}(\mathit{Predicates}^{\#})

The more versatile relation on abstract angels is the same as on
\mathit{Selections}
, except that the elements of the abstract selections are compared using the abstract more precise relation defined on abstract predicates.

Definition 0.

A\preceq_{a}^{\#}B\ \Leftrightarrow\ \forall b\in B\dv\exists\,a\in A\dv a\preceq_{d}^{\#}b

Definition 0.

The selection abstraction function
\alpha_{a}
relates elements of
\mathit{Selections}
to their abstract representation in
\mathit{Selections}^{\#}
.

\alpha_{a}:\mathit{Selections}\rightarrow\mathit{Selections}^{\#}

\alpha_{a}(R)=\{\alpha_{d}(r)\mid r\in R\}

The selection concretisation function
\gamma_{a}
relates elements of
\mathit{Selections}^{\#}
to the elements of the original domain
\mathit{Selections}
.

\gamma_{a}:\mathit{Selections}^{\#}\rightarrow\mathit{Selections}

\gamma_{a}(A)=\{\gamma_{d}(a)\mid a\in A\}

Lemma A.27.

R\preceq_{a}S\implies\alpha_{a}(R)\preceq_{a}^{\#}\alpha_{a}(S)\ .

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{d}(s)
be of
\alpha_{a}(S)
.
We know there is a predicate
r
in
R
more precise than
s
.
Thus, the inequality
\alpha_{d}(r)\preceq_{d}^{\#}\alpha_{d}(s)
is true.
And therefore the inequality

\alpha_{a}(R)\preceq_{a}^{\#}\alpha_{a}(S)

holds.
∎

Lemma A.28.

A\preceq_{a}B\implies\gamma_{a}(A)\preceq_{a}\gamma_{a}(B)\ .

Proof.

Let the predicate
\gamma_{d}(b)
be of
\gamma_{a}(B)
.
We know there is an abstract predicate
a
of
A
with
a\preceq_{d}^{\#}b
.
Thus,
\gamma_{d}(a)
is more precise than
\gamma_{d}(b)
.
Since
\gamma_{d}(a)\in\gamma_{a}(A)
, we know the inequality

\gamma_{a}(A)\preceq_{a}\gamma_{a}(B)

holds.
∎

Lemma A.29.

The pair
(\alpha_{a},\gamma_{a})
is a Galois connection between
\mathit{Selections}
and
\mathit{Selections}^{\#}
:

(\mathit{Selections},\preceq_{a})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.57222pt}{\hbox to29.79309pt{$\smash{-}\mkern-7.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\smash{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-7.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.01563pt}{\hbox to29.79309pt{$\mathord{\leftarrow}\mkern-7.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\smash{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-7.0mu\smash{-}$}}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\enskip\raisebox{-4.73608pt}{\ $\scriptstyle\alpha_{a}$\ }}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\smash{\hskip 5.49913pt\raisebox{7.50104pt}{\ $\scriptstyle\gamma_{a}$\ }}\hss}\rule[-5.73608pt]{0.0pt}{15.82043pt}\hskip 29.79309pt}(\mathit{Selections}^{\#},\preceq_{a}^{\#})

This means, the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\forall R\in\mathit{Selections}

\displaystyle\dv R\preceq_{a}\ \gamma_{a}(\alpha_{a}(R))

\displaystyle\forall\,A\in\mathit{Selections}^{\#}

\displaystyle\dv\alpha_{a}(\gamma_{a}(A))\preceq_{a}^{\#}\ A

Proof.

We start the proof with the first inequality.
Let
R
be of
\mathit{Selections}
.
Let the predicate
s
be of
\gamma_{a}(\alpha_{a}(R))
.
Then
s\in\{\gamma_{d}(a)\mid a\in\{\alpha_{d}(r)\mid r\in R\}\}
.
Thus,
s\in\{\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))\mid r\in R\}
.
Since the inequality
r\preceq_{d}^{\#}\gamma_{d}(\alpha_{d}(r))
is true, we get that there is an
r
in
R
more precise than
s
, thus the inequality
R\preceq_{a}^{\#}\gamma_{a}(\alpha_{a}(R))
holds.

Moving on with the second inequality, let
A
be of
\mathit{Selections}^{\#}
.
Let the abstract predicate
a
be of
A
.
We have
\alpha_{a}(\gamma_{a}(A))=\{\alpha_{d}(r)\mid r\in\{\gamma_{d}(a)\mid a\in A\}\}
.
Thus,
\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))
is in
\alpha_{a}(\gamma_{a}(A))
.
Since we know that the inequality

\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))\preceq_{d}^{\#}a
holds, we get that the inequality
\alpha_{a}(\gamma_{a}(A))\preceq_{a}^{\#}A
is true.
∎

Lemma A.30.

The pair
(\alpha_{d},\gamma_{d})
is a Galois insertion, i.e. the following equation holds for any
a
of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
:

\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))=a
.

The pair
(\alpha_{a},\gamma_{a})
also is a Galois insertion, i.e. the following equation holds true for any
A
of
\mathit{Selections}^{\#}
:

\alpha_{a}(\gamma_{a}(A))=A
.

Proof.

We start with the first equality.
Since we know the inequality
\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))\preceq_{d}^{\#}a
holds, it is sufficient to show that the inequality

a\preceq_{d}^{\#}\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))
is true because the relation
\preceq_{d}^{\#}
is antisymmetric.
So let
a
be of
\mathit{Predicates}^{\#}
.
If
a=\mathit{fail}
, then
\alpha_{d}(\gamma_{d}(a))
is also
\mathit{fail}
.
If
a
is not
\mathit{fail}
, then let
v
be any variable of
Vars
.
Then

\displaystyle a(v)\subseteq

\displaystyle\,\cup_{p\in\mathit{States}\wedge p(v)\subseteq a(v)}p(v)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\,\cup_{p\in\{p^{\prime}\in\mathit{States}\mid\forall v^{\prime}\in\textit{Vars}\dv p^{\prime}(v^{\prime})\subseteq a(v^{\prime})\}}p(v)

\displaystyle=
